115年 9月全國賦稅收入初步統計 
一、全國賦稅收入 
（一）本月數 
9 月實徵淨額 4,965 億元，較上年同月增加 813 億元(+19.6%)。 
（二）累計數 
累計 1 至 9 月實徵淨額 3 兆 5,714 億元，較上年同期增加 7,004 億
元(+24.4%)。 
（三）達成率 
1.中央政府總預算業於 9 月 18 日公布，稅課收入較原預算案提高
6,000 億元，併計地方政府及特種基金後，全國稅課收入較預算案
提高 5,852 億元，本次發布均改依法定數計算預算達成率(實徵淨
額占全年預算數)。 
2.累計 1 至 9 月實徵淨額占累計分配預算數 115.0%，占全年預算數
78.8%。

二、主要稅目別變動
（一）9 月實徵淨額與上年同月比較，以營業稅增加 297 億元、營利事業
所得稅增加 254 億元及證券交易稅增加 222 億元較多。
（二）累計 1 至 9 月實徵淨額與上年同期比較，以證券交易稅增加 3,077
億元、營利事業所得稅增加 1,853 億元及營業稅增加 1,203 億元較多。
圖 2 主要稅目別變化
(115 年 1-9 月)
1.營利事業所得稅
(1)9 月實徵淨額 2,007 億元，較上年同月增加 254 億元(+14.5%)，主
因暫繳稅款成長。
(2)累計 1 至 9 月實徵淨額 1 兆 28 億元，較上年同期增加 1,853 億元
(+22.7%)，主因結算申報自繳 、暫繳稅款 俱增及延分期稅款陸續
入帳。
2.綜合所得稅
9 月實徵淨額 490 億元，較上年同月增加 34 億元(+7.4%)，主因扣繳
稅款增加所致；累計 1 至 9 月實徵淨額 8,279 億元，較上年同期增加
790 億元(+10.6%)，主因結算申報自繳增加，以及盈餘分配、薪資所

得等扣繳稅款均呈成長。

4.貨物稅
(1)9月實徵淨額100億元，較上年同月減少11億元(-10.0%)，主因汽柴
油減徵幅度擴大，惟電動車大量到港推升進口 車稅額，抵銷部分
減幅。
(2)累計1至9月實徵淨額874億元，較上年同期減少207億元(-19.2%)，
除汽柴油降稅因素外 ，亦與新購汽機車及電動車之減免徵退稅增
加有關。
5.證券交易稅
(1)9 月及累計 1 至 9 月上市上櫃股票平均每日成交值為 1 兆 317 億
元、1 兆 1,461 億元，分別較上年同期(6,006 億元、4,451 億元)增
71.8%、1.6 倍。
(2)9 月 證 交 稅 實 徵 淨 額534 億 元， 較 上 年 同 月 增 加 222 億 元
(+71.2%)；累計 1 至 9 月實徵淨額 5,060 億元，較上年同期增加
3,077 億元(+1.6 倍)。
6.關稅
9 月實徵淨額 157 億元，較上年同月增加 3 億元(+1.9%)，累計 1 至 9
月實徵淨額 1,224 億元，較上年同期增加 80 億元(+7.0%)，均因機械

及電機設備、基本金屬及其製品等進口稅額增加。
7.贈與稅
9 月實徵淨額 23 億元，較上年同月增加 6 億元(+35.7%)，累計 1 至 9
月實徵淨額 211 億元，較上年同期增加 43 億元(+25.3%)，主因大額
稅款案件增加。
8.土地增值稅
9 月實徵淨額 56 億元，較上年同月增加 12 億元(+26.6%)，主因大額
稅款案件增加；累計 1 至 9 月實徵淨額 504 億元，較上年同期減少
14 億元(-2.8%)，主因房地交易量及大額稅款案件減少。
9.印花稅
9 月實徵淨額 31 億元，較上年同月增加 4 億元(+16.2%)，累計 1 至 9
月實徵淨額 175 億元，較上年同期增加 15 億元(+9.0%)，主因銀行放
款稅額成長。
（三）前 9月主要稅目實徵淨額占分配預算數之比率：
1.大於 100%者有證交稅(272%)、遺產稅(155%)、贈與稅(132%)、印花

## Document 154: Advanced economies _ Resilience to shock after shock (2026-09-24)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Advanced economies  _ Resilience to shock after shock (2026-09-24).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-24
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

3
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
History tends to repeat itself in advanced economies. Once again, growth ultimately fell short of expectations by only a small
margin in the first half of 2026, despite the conflict in Iran. As early as 2025, the impact of tariffs was less severe than feared.
This is a sign that structural factors (notably AI, defence and electrification) are underpinning growth in these countries

and are expected to continue to do so in 2027. However, there are significant risks to the economic outlook, primarily of a
geopolitical nature, with a significant upside risk to inflation and interest rates. The other instance of history repeating itself
concerns a period that those under 20 cannot have experienced: the period leading up to the 2008 crisis, echoes of which can
be seen in terms of growth, inflation, the direction of monetary policy, and long-term interest rates.

Advanced economies are experiencing trends similar to those they
faced nearly 20 years ago:
- Growth remains resilient in 2025-26 despite shocks, as it did in 2006-
07, in the major advanced economies.
- Inflation is above central banks’ targets: as in 2006-07, this calls for a
tightening of monetary policy.
- In Europe and the United States, long-term interest rates have re -
turned to their 2006-07 levels (1996 in Japan): a challenging environ-

ment for public finances in a context where public debt ratios are signi-
ficantly higher than those seen 20 years ago.
THE GLOBAL ECONOMY IN THE MIDST OF A GROWTH CYCLE
We are already well into 2026, and the picture so far is clear: glo -
bal growth is proving resilient. Following a US tariff shock last year,
growth is once again proving, for the time being, to be significantly less
affected by the conflict in Iran than had been anticipated when it first

broke out. Initially, however, there were fears that this new shock would
hit growth even harder. As with the tariff hike in 2025, the impact was
measurable, particularly in terms of consumer confidence. But in both
2025 and 2026, growth has also shown a degree of resilience.
The figures for the second quarter bear this out, despite the sharp rise
in oil prices, the recessionary impact of which is not yet apparent.
According to our forecasts, growth was expected to reach or exceed 1%

in 2026 in the four major advanced economies (the Eurozone, Japan,
the United Kingdom and the United States, where it exceeds 2%) for
the second consecutive year. This had not happened (excluding the
post-COVID catch-up period) since 2006-07. Growth figures are more
moderate (particularly for demographic reasons), but the private sec -
tor is not (at present) showing the same excesses (at that time, the
credit boom had preceded the worst crisis since the 1930s); although

debt levels have recently risen in the US tech sector.
The driving force behind the current growth cycle is clear: artificial
intelligence (AI). It is fuelling global trade and business investment
in most countries. This is clearly the case in the United States, where
business investment is growing strongly as investment plans in the
US tech sector are implemented.
However, the world’s major economies are currently engaged in a
broader push to expand their capital goods investment. Alongside the

ongoing development of services—particularly digital services—we
have seen a rebound in industrial production in most countries since
early 2025. The leading sectors are primarily technology equipment,
machinery, aeronautics, defence and their essential inputs (energy and
metals). This trend is evident everywhere, including in Europe, and
is becoming entrenched over the long term, with an improvement in
business climate indicators, particularly new export orders. Growth is

therefore expected to strengthen further in 2027, particularly in the
Eurozone, where German investment plans are set to gather momen -
tum and whose spillover effects will have a greater impact on the eco-
nomy.
However, not all sectors are experiencing the same momentum. This
is particularly true for the sectors that depend on household consump-
tion, which has shown mixed trends. Our observation that consumer
spending is more buoyant in the United States than in Europe was

confirmed in 2026, and the outlook does not point to a change in this
ranking.
EDITORIAL
RESILIENCE TO SHOCK AFTER SHOCK

EcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
The bank
for a changing
world
While European and US households are experiencing more or less the
same energy price shock, inflation remains higher in the United States.
But this has not prevented consumer spending from keeping pace, pri-
marily because it is driven by the wealthiest households, who are less
sensitive to inflation than to the wealth effect. Furthermore, while the

savings rate remains high in the Eurozone, US households have been
drawing down their savings. This is also true of British households,
which explains why the UK has seen slightly stronger growth since
the beginning of the year. In Japan, government spending has partially
offset energy inflation (while other countries are doing so to a much
lesser extent than in 2022), which has supported consumption growth.
However, these buffers—reduced savings or fiscal support—have their

limits when shocks persist. The two major current conflicts (Iran and
Ukraine) are far from being resolved, and their impact is continuing.
This persistent geopolitical risk and its implications for inflation repre-
sent a significant headwind to the growth outlook for 2027.
HIGHER INFLATION CALLS FOR MONETARY TIGHTENING
Inflation is exceeding the target set by three of the four central banks
examined in this publication, which is another similarity (albeit with

different underlying causes) with the 2006-07 period. Japan is the ex -
ception, as government support measures for households are tempo -
rarily holding back inflation.
Apart from the Bank of Japan, which is engaged in a long-term norma-
lisation process, no central bank was expected to raise its key rates
in 2026. But they are now being prompted to do so for reasons that
vary from one region to another. The first is the conflict in Iran and
the resulting rise in oil prices, which triggered a rebound in energy

inflation from the second quarter onwards. However, since the conflict
was not resolved by the memorandum of understanding signed in June,
and given that the subsequent drop in oil prices proved short-lived,
inflation is expected to rise again. The rise in gas prices is expected to
exacerbate the impact, particularly in Europe, where inventories are
being replenished ahead of winter. Electricity prices are expected to
follow suit in most countries, but at a more moderate pace than in

2022. Indeed, gas is now used less to generate electricity than it was
at that time (thanks to the expansion of renewable energy), and to an
even lesser extent in France.
There is a significant risk that energy inflation will spread further to
other components of inflation in the future, and inflation expectations
could eventually reflect this. This is why the European Central Bank
has already raised its key interest rate twice (in June and September)

and, according to our scenario, will likely do so again in December. For
the same reason, we expect the Bank of England to raise its policy rate
in November. Although the cap on energy prices was maintained for
a long time, it has now been raised. As a result, inflation is expected
to rise between now and the end of the year. The aim will then be to
prevent second-round effects.
Inflation can also be a sign of (too much) economic strength. In the

United States, it has exceeded the 2% target since 2021. The main
argument in favour of a new cycle of rate hikes (following the one in
2022-2023) lies in the current resilience of growth and the improving
labour market. The Federal Reserve (Fed) must balance two equally
important objectives: maximum employment and price stability. Howe-
ver, unlike in the 4 th quarter of 2025, when the decline in job crea -
tion justified monetary easing (by 75 basis points), the current sharp

rebound in job creation completely reverses that analysis. According
to our forecasts, the unemployment rate is expected to decrease in
the coming months, which pushes back the prospect of a decline in
core inflation. This situation persists due to both supply constraints
(over which a central bank has no control) and excess demand (which
the Fed can, however, mitigate). Consequently, the Fed raised its key
rate in September and is expected to implement two further rate hikes

between now and January 2027.
In Japan, finally, while headline inflation has been brought down by
government support measures, core inflation remains significant.
Wage pressures are mounting against a backdrop of labour shortages.
The Bank of Japan is therefore being forced to accelerate the pace of its
monetary normalisation.
In all these regions, inflation is currently expected to peak in Q4 2026
or Q1 2027. It is against this backdrop of temporary inflation that rate

hikes could also come to an end by that time (excluding Japan). Howe-
ver, this assumes that once the energy shock—the course of which
remains uncertain—subsides, inflation will fall. The effects of El Niño,
which are still difficult to gauge at present, could take over. Inflation
would then remain above the central banks’ target for a longer period.
Under this scenario, rate hikes would go further than is currently en -
visaged.
THE RISE IN LONG- TERM INTEREST RATES: A FACTOR THAT WILL WEIGH

ON PUBLIC FINANCES
Monetary policies have also to offset the inflationary effects of fiscal
policy, particularly in the United States (where the budget deficit is
high and shows no signs of shrinking) and in Japan (where the new
government is pursuing pro-growth policies). In Europe, fiscal policy
is playing a much smaller role than it did in 2022. While the stimulus
measures implemented at that time did help reduce the inflation rate
in the short term (by nearly 2.5 pp in France, for example), by keeping

demand at a high level, they delayed disinflation and increased the
scale of the monetary tightening required.
The outbreak of the conflict in Iran acted as a catalyst: it triggered a
rise in long-term rates (which had been trending downwards at the
start of the year) to levels close to those of 2006-07. This rise is wides-
pread, but its causes vary. Inflation expectations appear to play a more
significant role in the Eurozone, whereas the rise in real interest rates

seems to be the main factor in the United States and Japan.

The bank
for a changing
world
Higher inflation and more resilient real growth than feared point in
the same direction: stronger than expected nominal growth that is li -
kely to be sustained. However, in the long term, high nominal growth
tends to drive up nominal interest rates (as it is a positive sign of more
profitable investment projects), in contrast to the problem of secular

stagnation raised in the 2010s (which combined low nominal growth
with low nominal interest rates).
The other issue is the high level of fiscal deficits (too high for a period
of growth), with various situations. In several countries, the public
debt-to-GDP ratio continues to rise. In the United States, deficit reduc-
tion does not appear to be a priority, to the extent that it is expected
to remain above 6% of GDP at the very least until the end of Donald

Trump’s term of office (with little hope that the mid-term elections will
significantly alter this trend). In the United Kingdom and France, fiscal
consolidation has begun, but it is being hampered by the simultaneous
increase in certain areas of expenditure (debt servicing, defence and
healthcare, due in particular to an ageing population), which is slowing
the pace of consolidation. In Germany, the implementation of invest -
ment plans is leading to a widening of the deficit. While this does not

pose a sustainability problem, it does result in an increase in annual
debt issuance. In Japan, the budget deficit is kept in check by interest
payments that remain low, as the rise in interest rates is recent and
the average maturity of the debt is high (10 years). However, expansio-
nary fiscal policy could pose further problems in terms of debt dyna -
mics if it were to continue through the end of the decade. In Italy and
Spain, fiscal consolidation has been sufficient to stabilise the public

debt-to-GDP ratio (and even to reduce it in Spain), but the high level of
Italy’s debt service it vulnerable to a rise in interest rates.
In all these countries, the fiscal arithmetic is simpler today than it
will be in 2030. Indeed, in most cases, only half of the debt was is -
sued after 2022 (that is, after the end of the zero-interest-rate period).
The debt service has so far only partially risen, except in the United
States, where most of the increase has already occurred due to a shor-

ter average debt maturity (6 years). This means that, for all countries
combined, the rate of GDP growth remains higher than the average
interest rate paid on public debt (or the effective interest rate).
By 2030, the effective rate will have risen and caught up with
GDP growth. As a result, in most cases, the debt service will have re -
turned to the level as a share of GDP seen in the early 2000s. In order to
stabilise public debt-to-GDP ratios, primary balances will need to have

improved. This will even require the generation of primary surpluses,
particularly in Italy (which is already achieving this), France and the
United Kingdom. This is a crucial shift that governments will need to
introduce as early as the 2027 budget; otherwise, they risk postponing
the stabilisation of their public debt-to-GDP ratio once again.
Article completed on 17 September 2026
Stéphane Colliac
stephane.colliac@bnpparibas.com

## Document 178: August CPI – Came in on our expectations (2026-10-15)

Source PDF: all report pdf/August CPI – Came in on our expectations (2026-10-15).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-15
Pages: 16
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#inflation #australia #rba #economicbulletinsaustralia #inflation
August CPI – Came in on our expectations
By Justin Smirk & Neha Sharma
15:27 September 30 2026 Share
August Monthly CPI: Headline 0.4%mth/4.0%yr, Trimmed Mean 0.2%mth/3.6%yr. Market
services ex volatile –0.4%mth/3.3%yr.

The CPI rose 0.4% in August, spot on Westpac’s expectation but a bit softer than market
expectations of 0.5%. Due to base effects the annual pace picked up to 4.0%yr from 3.5%yr.

### Page 2

Key factors driving the headline result were auto fuels (14.8%); games, toys & hobbies (10.2%) with
rents providing a solid base (0.3%) while some offset was provided by both domestic (–4.9%) and
international holiday travel & accommodation (–1.5%).
Trimmed mean (TM) inflation printed 0.2% again bang on Westpac’s expectation while the market
was slightly higher at 0.3%. The annual pace was flat at 3.6%yr, the third month in a row at this pace,
while the six-month annualise pace was steady at 3.9%yr.

Key factors driving the TM print were: rents (0.3%) and new dwelling purchases (0.2%). The TM
distribution ranged from –0.38% to 0.82%.
Today’s inflation update revealed price discounting for some durable goods as retail demand softens.
We would expect to see a bounce from these categories in the following month. There was also
offsetting strength elsewhere in durable good so at least for now, the momentum continues to
suggest some near term upside risk to our current inflation estimates.

Nevertheless, as the TM came in on expectations and based on a first quick pass at updating the
quarterly profile, we see little pressure to revise our current Q3 estimates of 1.3%qtr for the CPI and
0.9%qtr for the TM.
The RBA remains alert to near term inflationary risks. We are expecting the momentum in TM
inflation to remain well above the top of the target band through to year-end underpinning our
expectations for the RBA to raise rates again in November.

### Page 3

August CPI in more detail
Following the bumper read in July, the monthly CPI moderated in August rising 0.4%mth. This was in
line with our expectations and slightly softer than the market consensus. This took the year-ended pace
to 4.0%yr. On a seasonally adjusted basis the CPI rose 0.7%mth highlighting the impact of seasonal
sales in August.

They key drivers behind the monthly lift were automotive fuel, up 14.8%mth, contributing 0.5ppts to the
headline figure. The surge resulted from the ending of the fuel excise discount on August 3 and rising
global energy prices. With crude oil prices still elevated due to the conflict in the middle east, and
growing shortages of refined products due to both the middle east conflict and Ukrainian assaults on
Russian refining capacity, auto fuel is expected to remain an inflationary impulse at least until the end
of 2026.

The monthly TM also softened, rising 0.2%mth compared to the 0.5%mth read from July. The result
was again in line with our expectations, and very close at two decimal places (0.24%mth vs
0.23%mth), but softer than the market's view. The year-ended pace held at 3.6%yr for the third
consecutive month, although it continued to ease marginally at the second decimal place. In contrast,
the six-month annualised pace has continued to trend higher since April and is currently running at
3.9%yr.

### Page 4

There is a possibility that as the ABS continues to grapple with seasonal adjustment issues we will see
revisions to the TM estimates. Last July also saw a strong 0.48%mth read which eased to 0.29%mth in
August. This is not too dissimilar from the 0.48%mth and 0.24%mth reads for 2026.

We are expecting core inflation to start to moderate as we get to year end but our current estimate for
the month of December still has the TM running at a 3.3% in the year while the six-month annualised
pace is still a chunky 3.1%. Applying a trim on the seasonally adjusted 3m/3m expenditure classes
yields a TM of 0.9%qtr currently. This is in line with our current Q3 TM estimate.

Other measures of underlying inflation told a similar story, easing on a monthly basis but holding steady
on a year-ended basis. Market services ex volatiles fell –0.4%mth to be 3.3%yr higher. Excluding both
volatiles & holidays saw a 0.2%mth/4.1%yr lift.

Most market services components eased in the month (including on a seasonally adjusted basis) but
annual growth across this group as a whole remained above the RBA's 2-3% target band. The near-
term momentum (three-month annualised) is also quite strong across several components of services.
Of note include deposit and loan facilities (11.2%mth), sports participation (10.9%), clothing repair
(7.0%), restaurant meals (6.8%) and other household services (5.2%). We are closely watching market
services as this is where inflationary risks, outside of energy, appear to be most persistent.

Due to its sizable weight in the CPI, and the fact price changes here tend to be less volatile than many
other categories, so they almost always appear in the TM estimates, housing prices remain a key
focus. Rents rose 0.3%mth in August, in line with our expectations, to be up 3.6%yr. Since April, the
seasonally adjusted rents series has been running lower than the non-seasonally adjusted series. We
are expecting rents to hold around this pace till year end before moderating modestly as we move
through 2027.

New dwellings inflation was 0.2%mth in August (softer than our expectation for a 0.4%mth rise). Since
peaking in May, monthly price growth has slowed in each successive month, potentially reflecting
increasing difficulty for developers in passing through higher costs amid softer conditions in established
housing markets and weaker demand in the face of higher interest rates. The annual pace looks to
have topped out. It was at 5.8%yr in June easing to 5.4%yr currently with the three-month annualised
pace at 3.7%. We expect dwelling price inflation to continue easing through to the end of 2027 as
falling established house prices limit the ability of builders to pass on rising construction costs. There is
a medium-term risk that as conditions improve builders may raise prices more aggressively to claw
back margins.

### Page 5

Overall, the breadth of inflation moderated in August. The share of the basket running above 3%yr
eased from 45% to 42%, with the share below 2%yr increasing from 38% to 41%.

### Page 6

Key surprises in the month:
Upside surprises

Downside surprises
Insurance surprised to the upside (0.7%mth vs 0.3%mth) after a run of weaker-than-usual reads over
the past two months.
Motor vehicle repair costs rose a solid 0.8% against our 0.3%mth expectations, with spare parts and
accessories also up 0.3%mth vs –0.4%mth.
Footwear rose 1.7%mth against our expectations for a -2.9%mth decline, the gains were broad-
based across women, men & children.
Household goods surprised to the upside. Household appliances, utensils and tools rose 1.9%mth
against our flat expectations, meanwhile household textiles fell –1.0%mth against our expectations
for a –5.0%mth fall. Furniture and furnishings also fell by less than expected –0.6%mth vs –1.3%mth.
Recreation and culture surprised to the upside (–1.0%mth vs –1.5%mth), owing to a 1.4%mth lift in
other recreation, sport & culture against our expectations for a flat read on the back of an outsized
gain in games, toys & hobbies (10.2%mth vs 2.5%mth). Holiday travel was marginally less weak than
expected (–3.3% vs –3.5%mth).

### Page 7

Food was the key downside miss falling –0.1%mth against our expectations for a 0.2%mth lift. Meat
& seafood (–0.9%mth vs 0.5%mth) and fruit & vegetables (–1.2%mth vs 0.1%mth) were the key
areas of weakness.
Audio, visual and computing equipment and services came in weaker-than-expected (0.7%mth vs
1.2%mth), owing to a softer 0.6%mth lift in computing services (3.0%mth expected). This was partly
balanced by a stronger than expected lift in computing equipment (0.9%mth vs –0.5%mth). The latter

is where we are most likely to see the impact of the AI boom in the CPI, due to computer chip
inflation, other than construction cost inflation for new dwellings.
Motor vehicles came in lower at –0.4%mth vs –0.1%mth which could be the start of price pressures
associated with the rapid expansion of the share of Chinese cars in the Australian market.
Automotive fuel was also slightly softer at 14.8%mth vs 16.7%mth.
Garments and accessories were both softer. Garments fell –1.9%mth against our expectations for a

0.2%mth rise, meanwhile accessories came in at 0.1%mth vs 0.5%mth expected.

### Page 8

What does this mean for the RBA?
Yesterday, post the RBA rate hike and monetary policy decision statement, Westpac changed its view
from a November follow up rate hike being a risk to being the base case. The post-meeting statement
was notable for its emphasis on the Middle East conflict, noting that “global energy prices are now
much higher than had been assumed in the August forecasts”. In addition, RBA’s liaison suggests firms
are passing this on or planning to do so. The pass-through was characterised as partial, and additional

to domestically driven inflation arising from capacity pressures. We read this as implying that the RBA
is concerned about upside risks to underlying inflation in Q4 of this year, as well as Q3.

### Page 9

Related articles
Our current forecast of 0.9%qtr for the September quarter TM stands post today’s release and
represents a step up from 0.8% in the June quarter holding the annual pace at 3.6%yr. Our current
forecast for the Q4 TM is 0.7%qtr/3.3%yr but given the move in energy prices, and anecdotes of firms
now passing on those rising costs, we too see upside risks to this estimate.

As such, we hold to the view that the RBA will follow up with another rate hike in November. For more
information on the RBA decision please see Luci Ellis’s note “RBA hikes in September, November
follow-up now expected".

3

Lợi nhuận sau thuế công ty mẹ tăng 
trưởng khả quan trong 2Q26 14% 
YoY, với động lực chính đến từ mảng 
dịch vụ CNTT nước ngoài

Mảng CNTT nước ngoài: Lĩnh vực dịch 
vụ IT phân cực mạnh mẽ do tác nhân 
AI (AI Agentic) s ẽ củng c ố xu hư ớng 
đầu tư vào AI

FPT ở vị thế thuận lợi cho phép 
doanh nghiệp đa dạng hóa công cụ 
AI và tối ưu chi phí vận hành

Khuyến ngh ị MUA với giá m ục tiêu 
86,000 VND/cổ phiếu 
 Do không còn h ạch toán FPT Telecom (FOX) là công ty con, d oanh thu 2Q2026 của 
FPT giảm mạnh còn 13,810 tỷ VND (-17% YoY). Lợi nhuận sau thuế công ty mẹ không 
bị ảnh hưởng bởi thay đổi hạch toán đạt 2,568 tỷ VND (14% YoY). Mảng dịch vụ CNTT 
tại thị trường nước ngoài tiếp tục là đ ộng lực tăng trư ởng chính, đạt doanh thu và 
LNTT lần lượt là 9,868/1,538 tỷ VND (16.3% /19.3% YoY).

Sự chuyển dịch chi tiêu từ dịch vụ IT truyền thống sang chuyển đổi số và tích hợp AI 
được kỳ vọng sẽ diễn ra ngày m ột mạnh mẽ khi: i) Sự phát triển của năng l ực tính 
toán giúp kéo giảm chi phí token (đơn v ị năng lực tính toán của AI) và thúc đ ẩy nhu 
cầu sử dụng AI của doanh nghiệp; ii) Các mô hình AI mã nguồn mở gia tăng lựa chọn 
cho người dùng sẽ thúc đẩy chi tiêu

Đối với các doanh nghiệp ứng dụng sâu các mô hình AI phục vụ hoạt động kinh doanh 
như FPT , chúng tôi nhận thấy xu hướng tối ưu chi phí tốt hơn nhờ khả năng sử dụng 
các mô hình AI đ ến từ đa dạng nhà cung c ấp; với chi phí trên hi ệu năng tính toán 
được cải thi ện. Trong k ịch b ản cơ s ở, chúng tôi d ự báo biên l ợi nhuận trước thu ế 
mảng CNTT nước ngoài sẽ ổn định ở mức 15.4% nhờ duy trì chi ến lược quản trị chi 
phí hiệu quả qua đó giúp LNTT 2026/2027 đạt 6,198/7,087 tỷ VND (13.3%/14.3% YoY).

Dựa trên định giá FCFF và P/E, triển vọng kinh doanh cũng như xem xét các rủi ro có 
thể phát sinh, chúng tôi khuyến nghị MUA đối với cổ phiếu FPT . Giá mục tiêu là 86,000 
VND/cổ phiếu.

Các mảng kinh doanh duy trì ổn định 
giúp lợi nhuận sau thuế công ty mẹ 
tăng trưởng nhẹ

Sản lượng điện của REE tăng trưởng 
nhẹ nhờ mức tăng của mảng điện gió 
sau khi dự án Duyên Hải V1-4 vận 
hành

Mảng M&E ghi nhận giá trị ký mới cao 
trong 1H.2026, t ạo kỳ vọng hồi phục 
mạnh mẽ doanh thu

Mảng cho thuê văn phòng đứng trước 
cơ hội lớn đóng góp vào tăng trư ởng 
nhờ các điều kiện thuận lợi

Khuyến nghị MUA với giá mục tiêu 
58,100 VNĐ/cổ phiếu

Doanh thu Quý 2/2026 giảm nhẹ, đạt 2,432 tỷ VND (-3% YoY/-2% QoQ), trong khi l ợi 
nhuận sau thuế công ty mẹ đạt 632 tỷ VND (+1% YoY/-11.5% QoQ), do doanh thu của 
mảng bất động sản suy giảm. Qua đó, REE hoàn thành lần lượt 40%/47.8% kế hoạch 
doanh thu và LNST công ty mẹ 2026.

Trong Q2.2026, tổng sản lượng phát đi ện của REE tăng nh ẹ, đạt 2.8 tỷ kWh (+2.3% 
YoY) với đóng góp tăng trưởng rõ nét đến từ mảng điện gió khi dự án Duyên Hải V1-4 
bắt đầu vận hành; tuy nhiên  sản lượng thủy điện suy gi ảm do tác đ ộng tiêu c ực từ 
pha thời tiết El Nino, đạt 1.1 tỷ kWh.

Với năng lực chuyên môn được nâng cao, mảng M&E ghi nhận những dấu hiệu phục 
hồi tích cực trong giá trị hợp đồng ký mới trong 1H.2026, 5,865 tỷ VND (cao hơn 53.9% 
so với giá trị ký mới cả năm 2025). KBSV dự phóng doanh thu c ủa mảng M&E sẽ đạt 
4,499/5,190 tỷ VND (+28%/15% YoY) trong 2026/2027.

Hội tụ bởi 3 yếu tố: i) Định hướng phát triển kinh tế Hồ Chí Minh trở thành trung tâm 
tài chính quốc tế; ii) Hạ tầng giao thông ngày một được hoàn thiện và iii) Chi phí thuê 
cạnh tranh so với chất lượng dịch vụ của E. Town 6, KBSV dự phóng doanh thu mảng 
cho thuê văn phòng trong 2026/2027 tăng trưởng +14%/6.2% YoY.

Dựa trên đ ịnh giá SOTP , triển vọng kinh doanh cũng như xem xét các r ủi ro có th ể 
phát sinh, chúng tôi khuy ến nghị MUA đối với cổ phiếu REE. Giá m ục tiêu là 58,100 
VND/cổ phiếu, cao hơn 28.3% so với mức giá đóng cửa tại ngày 15/09/2026.

Doanh thu 2Q.26 bứt phá nhờ khoản 
thu nhập bất thường của nhiệt điện 
Cà Mau và Vũng Áng

Nhiệt đi ện than duy trì công su ất ở 
mức cao nh ờ nguồn cung nhiên li ệu 
ổn định trong 2H26

Điện khí Đông Nam Bộ (NT1&2 và 
NT3&4) có triển vọng hoạt động 
thuận lợi

Khuyến nghị MUA với giá mục tiêu 
18,100 VND/cổ phiếu 
 Trong Quý 2/2026, POW ghi nhận Doanh thu và LNST cổ đông công ty mẹ đạt lần lượt 
20,308 tỷ VND (116.1% YoY) và 3,519 tỷ VND (484.6% YoY) chủ yếu nhờ các khoản thu 
nhập từ chênh lệch tiền khí của nhiệt điện Cà Mau và tỷ giá của nhiệt điện Vũng Áng. 
Với kết qu ả trên, POW hoàn thành l ần lư ợt 65%/420% kế hoạch doanh thu và l ợi 
nhuận sau thuế công ty mẹ.

Chúng tôi cho r ằng nhà máy nhi ệt than Vũng Áng trong 2026 s ẽ được duy trì công 
suất huy động ở mức cao nhờ: i) Xác suất diễn ra El Nino tăng cao và dự kiến kéo dài 
đến đầu 2027, ii) Nguồn cung than trong nước được bảo đảm từ các đối tác (TKV, TCT 
Đông B ắc) và d ự trữ than d ồi dào c ủa doanh nghi ệp ghi nh ận ở mức cao trong 
Q2/2026.

Dự án đường dây 500kV Mạch 3 đi vào vận hành giúp giảm áp lực cạnh tranh của các 
nhà máy điện tại khu vực phía Nam, qua đó kỳ vọng doanh thu của NT1&2 trong năm 
2026 đạt 11,104 tỷ VND (5.3% YoY). Đối với dự án điện NT3&4, khoản thu nhập chênh 
lệch sản lượng theo giá công suất do sản lượng điện thực tế thấp hơn so với tỷ lệ Qc 
65% của nhà máy được duy trì sang 2H26 và 2027 sẽ giúp cho dự án ghi nhận doanh 
thu lần lượt 17,848/17,621 tỷ VND doanh thu trong 2026/2027.

Dựa trên định giá SOTP và EV/EBITDA, tri ển vọng kinh doanh cũng như xem xét các 
rủi ro có th ể phát sinh, chúng tôi khuy ến nghị MUA đối với cổ phiếu POW. Giá m ục 
tiêu là 18,100 VND/cổ phiếu.

động lực chủ yếu đến từ (1) đóng góp từ 19 điểm kinh doanh mở mới tại T3 Tân Sơn 
Nhất và (2) tăng trưởng tích cực của các mảng hàng miễn thuế, nhà hàng - fastfood 
và phòng ch ờ thương gia. Lũy k ế 6T2026, doanh thu và LNST đ ạt 1,035/221 t ỷ VND 
(+33%/46% yoy), lần lượt hoàn thành 48%/49% kế hoạch năm.

Chúng tôi dự phóng lượng khách quốc tế/nội địa tại các thị trường AST khai thác tăng 
bình quân 11%/5% mỗi năm đến 2030. Lưu lượng và tỷ trọng khách quốc tế gia tăng 
được kỳ vọng thúc đẩy số giao dịch và doanh thu bình quân trên mỗi khách, đặc biệt 
tại mảng miễn thuế, bán lẻ và phòng chờ.

Chúng tôi dự phóng số điểm kinh doanh AST tăng từ 145 điểm cuối năm 2026 lên 182 
điểm năm 2030. Sau đóng góp t ừ 19 điểm tại T3 Tân Sơn Nh ất và 6 điểm mới tại T2 
Nội Bài từ 2025 - 2026, các dự án Long Thành, Phú Qu ốc và Gia Bình ti ếp tục mở ra 
dư địa mở rộng mạng lưới trong trung và dài hạn cho doanh nghiệp.

Việc mở rộng các hoạt động mang lại lợi nhuận cao, bao gồm hàng miễn thuế, phòng 
chờ thương gia và tối ưu hoạt động tại các điểm kinh doanh đ ược kỳ vọng tác động 
tích c ực lên biên l ợi nhu ận của AST . Chúng tôi dự phóng biên l ợi nhu ận g ộp đ ạt 
64.7%/65.5% trong năm 2026/2027, hỗ trợ LNST công ty mẹ tăng lần lượt 48%/17% 
yoy.

Khuyến nghị MUA với giá mục tiêu 
59,000 VNĐ/cổ phiếu

Doanh thu 2Q2026 đạt 1,222 tỷ (-8% YoY/18.1% QoQ) và LNST công ty mẹ đạt 325 tỷ 
VND (7% YoY/55% QoQ), ghi nhận tăng trưởng của tất cả các mảng. Mảng cung cấp 
nước tăng trưởng ổn định, tiếp tục là trụ cột chính của BWE, đóng góp doanh thu 785 
tỷ VND (+12% YoY/12% QoQ) nhờ nhà máy nước Long An v ận hành từ Q3/2025 giúp 
sản lượng nước tăng 28% YoY.

Cường độ và thời gian kéo dài c ủa các đ ợt nắng nóng trong 2026/2027 là cơ s ở để 
chúng tôi đi ều chỉnh tăng tăng trư ởng tiêu th ụ nước của nhóm khách hàng dân cư 
tại Bình Dương và Bình Phư ớc trong năm 2026 - 2027 lên mức 7.5/6.5% YoY. Trái lại, 
triển vọng tăng trưởng tiêu thụ nước nhóm sản xuất ảm đạm do tình hình thuế quan 
có diễn biến bất lợi trong ngắn hạn cho các ngành xu ất khẩu truyền thống của Việt 
Nam.

UBND TP .HCM công bố đơn giá thu gom, vận chuyển và xử lý rác thải mới, tăng mạnh 
từ 50-150% nhằm phản ánh đầy đủ và chính xác hơn các chi phí phát sinh trong thực 
tế. Chúng tôi cho rằng điều này sẽ tác động tích cực với BWE khi các dự án của doanh 
nghiệp ở mảng này s ẽ vận hành hi ệu quả hơn. Dự báo doanh thu và l ợi nhuận gộp 
của mảng xử lý rác thải lần lượt đạt 1,069/1,234 và 165.7/180.7 tỷ VND lần lượt trong 
2026/2027, tương ứng mức tăng 34%/15% và 27%/9% YoY.

hình thành cây nến tuần giảm điểm nhưng với phần bóng dưới khá dài cho thấy sự tham 
gia tích cực của lực cầu bắt đáy tại vùng hỗ trợ gần. Trên khung ngày, nhịp giảm mạnh giữa 
tuần đã khiến chỉ số đánh mất đáy ngắn hạn tuần trước và chớm cắt xuống dưới SMA200, 
trong khi phiên hồi phục cuối tuần chưa cho thấy sự cải thiện rõ rệt của lực cầu. Mặc dù 
diễn biến này cho thấy áp lực điều chỉnh vẫn có thể còn tiếp diễn, tuy nhiên khi xu hướng 
tăng ngắn hạn chưa bị phá vỡ, VNINDEX được kỳ vọng sẽ nhận được lực đỡ tại vùng hỗ trợ 
gần quanh 1750, tương ứng vùng hợp lưu quanh SMA50, và có cơ hội hồi phục trở lại.

## Document 201: Capital Market Outlook (2026-10-13)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Capital Market Outlook (2026-10-13).pdf
Report source: Merrill Lynch (CMO)
Date: 2026-10-13
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

CHIEF INVESTMENT OFFICE
Capital Market Outlook

### Page 2

2 of 8 October 13, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Consumers Feel One Way, but Spend Another
Kirsten Cabacungan, Vice President and Senior Investment Strategist
Americans have spent much of the past several years telling surveys they are cautious
about the economy. Yet they continue to spend as though they are not. The University
of Michigan’s Consumer Sentiment Index remains near multidecade lows, while the
Conference Board’s Index of Consumer Confidence has also been in a yearslong downtrend.

These surveys reflect a list of persistent concerns from elevated food and gas prices to
geopolitical uncertainty and labor market anxiety.
Even so, consumer spending has remained remarkably resilient. The final estimate of Q2
real consumer spending, as measured by PCE, was revised up to an annualized rate of
3.8%, well above the 2.3% 20-year average. More recent data suggest that momentum
has continued. According to Bank of America Institute, the latest monthly total credit and

debit card spending data per household rose 6.2% year-over-year (YoY) in September, and
spending excluding gasoline was up 5.1% YoY, both the third strongest growth rates in the
past four years. 1
The persistence of consumer spending is particularly notable given that temporary cyclical
boosts like larger tax refunds are starting to fade. That raises an important question: What is
actually funding strong consumption today? Recent data suggest the drivers are becoming

increasingly more diversified, with broadening labor market trends, healthy consumer
finances and elevated wealth effects all reinforcing one another and underpinning consumers.
First, the labor market continues to provide an important foundation for consumer spending,
and recent trends suggest wage growth is supporting a broader set of households than in
recent years. Jobs growth has slowed from the exceptionally strong pace seen following the
pandemic, but broader indicators suggest labor market conditions remain relatively stable.

Although the September jobs report came in softer than expected, the three- and six-month
rolling averages indicate that the trend in jobs growth likely bottomed out last year and has
gradually improved since (Exhibit 1A). Meanwhile, the unemployment rate remains low by
historical standards and layoffs remain contained. And while wage growth has moderated
and recently trailed headline inflation (Exhibit 1B), cumulative income gains over the past
several years have likely helped many households absorb higher prices better.

An important development though has been improving wage growth trends among lower-
income households, where spending tends to be most sensitive to changes in wages. Bank
of America Institute consumer deposit account data show after-tax wage growth for lower-
income households outpaced higher-income households for a second consecutive month
in August, rising 4.7% YoY compared with 3.5% for higher-income households. Importantly,
this improvement appears to be supporting discretionary spending, not just spending

on necessities. Lower-income discretionary spending rose 5.7% YoY in August, nearly
matching the 5.9% pace for higher-income households. In other words, the narrowing gap
in wage and spending growth across income cohorts suggests consumer resilience looks
to be becoming less dependent on affluent households alone, creating a broader and more
durable foundation for consumption.
Second, household finances remain in relatively good shape, providing consumers with the

flexibility to continue spending. The ratio of household debt to disposable personal income
remains near its lowest level since the late 1990s, excluding the temporary distortions
caused by pandemic-related income support in 2020 and 2021 (Exhibit 1C). Recent
revisions to the Bureau of Economic Analysis national income accounts also suggest
consumers have a larger financial buffer than previous data suggested. Personal saving
estimates over the past year have been revised higher by $320 billion, or 39%, and the

revised savings rate remains above 4% after previous estimates showed it fell to 3% during
the summer.2 Concerns that consumers may be rapidly exhausting their savings buffers
may therefore be overstated. Supporting this view, Bank of America Institute consumer
1 Bank of America Institute, “Consumer Checkpoint: Strength in numbers.” As of October 9, 2026.
2 Bank of America, “Massive Savings Markup = Another Reason to Be Bullish.” As of October 2, 2026.

Investment Implications
Consumer spending strength
remains a key pillar of economic
and earnings growth, which keeps
us constructive on our Equity
overweight. The growing connection
between household wealth and
spending reinforces the importance
of maintaining a diversified portfolio.

### Page 3

3 of 8 October 13, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
deposit account data show that middle- and lower-income households continue to maintain
deposit balances above prepandemic levels across age groups.
Third, elevated household wealth continues to provide a powerful tailwind for consumer
spending, particularly among households that account for an outsized share of overall
consumption. U.S. household net worth rose $12.8 trillion (T) to a record $195.9T in Q2,

driven largely by gains in Equities, which contributed $10.7T of the increase (Exhibit 1D).
As a result, household net worth relative to disposable income climbed to a record 8.3,
meaning households now hold more than eight dollars of wealth for every dollar of annual
disposable income. Households are also sitting on $35.8T in home equity, an all-time high
level that provides a significant balance sheet cushion. Rising asset values have helped to

support spending through what economists refer to as wealth effects. In other words, when
households feel wealthier, they tend to be more willing to spend.
For investors, the key takeaway is that consumer spending is being supported by multiple
engines despite the weaker sentiment data. Labor income trends are supporting wider
participation, healthy balance sheets are helping households absorb economic pressures,
and elevated household wealth continues to sustain higher-income spending. Together,

these forces are supportive of economic growth and corporate profits, which is particularly
important since the consumer accounts for roughly 70% of U.S. gross domestic product.
That said, it does not eliminate risk. A meaningful risk to the consumer would be a scenario
in which both the labor and wealth engines weaken simultaneously. For now, labor market
conditions remain stable, and wealth effects remain supportive. Equity market pullbacks in
recent years have done little to derail consumer spending, suggesting it would likely take a

deeper and more prolonged drawdown to alter consumer behavior.
Exhibit 1: Consumer Spending is Being Supported by Multiple Engines.
A) Jobs growth has stablized. B) Wage growth have helped offset higher prices.

4 of 8 October 13, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
The Bull Market Turns Four: Older, Unloved—But Still Kicking
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Ariana Chiu, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
Four years ago this month, virtually no one was in the mood to buy U.S. Equities, and for
good reasons.
Rewind to October 2022: Inflation the month before clocked in at an annual rate of 8.2%.

The yield curve was inverted, a signal that has preceded most past recessions. Consumer
sentiment was abysmal, having dropped to a record low in June 2022. Recession
concerns were mounting, with an October Wall Street Journal poll survey putting the
odds of a recession within 12 months at about 63%. And realizing (belatedly) that
inflation was not “transitory,” the Fed was in the midst of administering one of the most
aggressive monetary tightening cycles in decades, raising the fed funds rate five times

between March and September 2022 by a stunning 300 basis points.
Reflecting the mood at the moment, the S&P 500 closed at 3,577 on October 12, 2022,
down 25% below its January peak, leaving investors battered and bruised but blind to the
fact that the market had bottomed. Four years on, the bull market that emerged from
the gloom of 2022 is still standing and kicking, with the S&P 500 up roughly 130% since
the October 2022 lows.
The more than doubling of the S&P over the past four years has hardly been linear—and

has been accompanied with plenty of pain and doubt. Exhibit 2 maps the rocky path
trodden by the bull over the past four years. Less than six months into the rally, the
markets were rocked by the failure of Silicon Valley Bank (SVB) on March 10, 2023. The
S&P Regional Banks Select Industry Index lost 18% that week and stoked concerns of
massive deposit runs and credit stress across the capital markets. However, owing to
emergency intervention by the government, lower yields and strength elsewhere in the

markets, the crisis passed. Some banks failed that month, but the bull market didn’t.
Exhibit 2: Revisiting the Rocky Path Trodden by the Bull.
Period "Reason to Sell" What happened instead
October 2022 Inflation >8%, aggressive Fed tightening, recession concerns Bull market begins
March 2023 SVB/regional banking crisis Stocks keep climbing
2023-24 Inverted yield curve, “recession inevitable” Economy keeps expanding
2024 “Magnificent 7* bubble,” narrow breadth, expensive valuations Earnings propel market higher

2025 Tariffs, trade war, policy uncertainty Market absorbs another shock
2026 Oil/diesel shock, 5%+ yields, Fed tightening, geopolitical risks Bull reaches fourth birthday
*Apple, Amazon, Alphabet, Nvidia, Meta, Microsoft and Tesla. Source: Chief Investment Office. As of October 9, 2026.
But recessionary concerns hung like threatening clouds over the markets for most of
2023. Aggressive Fed tightening, an inverted yield curve, and soft economic data were

all taken as harbingers of an economic slowdown and profits recession. Yet the S&P 500
continued to climb in 2023, up 26.3% for the year, driven in large part by the outsized
price performance of the Magnificent Seven.
Investors spent much of 2024 fretting about a market too dependent on technology,
and therefore a market too narrow and too expensive. The unwinding of the Japan carry
trade in August 2024 engendered more angst, as did uncertainties around the 2024

U.S. presidential election. But despite a host of misgivings, the S&P 500 delivered total
returns of 25% in 2024. The unloved bull marched on.
Total returns in 2025 were robust as well—roughly 18%—notwithstanding—yet
again—a number of headwinds. In late January, DeepSeek, a cheaper China AI model,
raised questions about America’s technology leadership and the need to spend enormous
sums on chips and data centers. The Nasdaq promptly dropped by 3.1% thereafter.

Investment Implications
The adoption phase of the AI buildout
should translate to broader earnings
growth and performance across
sectors, reinforcing our conviction
in U.S. Equities. Given a strong
fundamental backdrop, we would
continue to buy on weakness as the
bull enters its fifth year

### Page 5

5 of 8 October 13, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
January’s jolt was followed by “Liberation Day,” or the warning of punishing U.S. tariffs
on America’s major trading partners. The trade-policy shock of April 2025 caused the
S&P 500 to drop nearly 19% from its February high to its April 8 low on uncertainty
that higher import costs would decrease corporate margins and consumer spending and
upend global supply chains. Amid the trade tumult, Treasury prices fell and the U.S. dollar

weakened on concerns that foreigners were abandoning U.S. assets. Remember all the
angst over the dollar debasement trade? If that wasn’t enough, expensive valuations and
enormous capital commitments raised doubts about the AI trade and triggered talk of an
AI bubble. Yet despite this wall of worry, the S&P 500, for the third year running, posted
double-digit gains in 2025.
And double-digit gains could be in store this year, with the S&P 500 up 14% through

October 9. The bull has rumbled on, taking in stride the energy shock from the war in
the Middle East, the shift in interest rate expectations from the Fed (from rate cuts
to rate hikes), and the rising uncertainty associated with the midterm elections. These
headwinds have been countered by tailwinds such as better-than-expected corporate
earnings and better-than-anticipated real growth.
Finally, as Exhibit 3 makes clear, what has given this bull so much muscle has been

the U.S. Technology sector, specifically the Magnificent 7. Powering the bull has been
the Magnificent 7, with a staggering 344% return since the 2022 lows, while U.S.
technology has delivered a 277% return versus the S&P 500’s return of 130%. Note the
outperformance of the S&P 500 relative to the main international indices.
Looking forward: Is the bull’s greatest strength also its Achilles’ heel? Bull
markets don’t die of natural causes. And they typically don’t expire because of one

particular signal or indicator. Bull markets turn to bear markets when a cluster of signals/
metrics turn south. Think deteriorating and downward earnings revisions, higher interest
rates, widening credit spreads and tighter lending standards, growth disappointments,
stretched valuations and sentiment, deteriorating market breadth, and an external
shock. Any one of these signals could flash red and not trouble the bull.  But when they
converge or feed off each other, that’s trouble.

In addition, since a great deal of the earnings growth, market leadership and capital
expenditures (capex) spending of this bull market have come from the technology sector,
the bull’s greatest strength could become its greatest vulnerability. AI has become an
investment ecosystem unto itself, and hence the fifth year of the bull market will pivot
in part around AI revenue, frontier model adoption rates, infrastructure spending, free
cash flows, depreciation costs, debt issuance, data center utilization, cloud growth, and

competition via cheaper models and computing alternatives. To put it mildly, there is a
great deal at stake when it comes to the bull and AI.
However, this is where we stand today: We believe the bull will continue to rumble
ahead next year, with AI revenues and productivity gains justifying the costs of the AI
infrastructure. Critically, AI is moving from the buildout phase, dominated by chipmakers
and hyperscaler capex, into the adoption phase, where software firms, healthcare,

finance, energy and a myriad of other firms start to see productivity gains and margin
expansion from rising AI adoption rates. We believe this will give better breadth to the
S&P 500; so too will continued profit growth across various U.S. sectors, AI investment
that starts to pay off, and an economy capable of absorbing setbacks or periodic bouts
of uncertainty.
The path forward won’t be linear—and there will be fits and starts, and market
drawdowns. That’s normal for any market. But that aside, the bull will keep rumbling,

roaming and kicking in 2027.

### Page 6

6 of 8 October 13, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THE TICKER TAPE
What is our view of the macro and market landscape heading
into 2027 and beyond?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investment Office
A supply side-led economic expansion continues, driven by capital investment in the AI
buildout, solid consumer spending and fiscal tailwinds still blowing, albeit with less force.
This creates a backdrop that keeps nominal growth well-above trend and, importantly,

above the interest cost on national debt.
The interest rate backdrop largely remains the same as of today, but, instead of the
sharp increases that we experienced in 2026, the curve should settle down, and yields
should remain relatively range bound. The Fed is likely to tap on the breaks a couple of
more times before reassessing new inflation, growth and employment trends later in
2027.
Concerns surrounding credit risk and "circular" financing are likely to rise the further we

move into the AI buildout, but we do not expect a default cycle to develop. Rather, we
expect the sharp backup in yields to place pressure namely on lower-quality credits in
areas having a difficult time building the cash flow necessary to pay the higher interest
costs.
We expect a broadening out within the market but technology to still maintain its
heavyweight positioning. As we move from training and evolving the large language
models to agentic, and physical AI new leaders are likely to develop. In addition, we

anticipate AI adopters such as biotechnology, transport, logistics, advance manufacturing
and financial companies to enter new growth and productivity phases.
Lastly, overall earnings growth in the U.S. should be low to mid double-digit percent
stages in 2027, which is about half of the growth from 2026 thus far but still around
double from normal levels.
Credit risks developing and higher energy prices for longer are the main risks to our bull
case for Equities extending further into 2027 and beyond.

Index Definitions
ecurities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

University of Michigan’s Consumer Sentiment Index is a monthly survey of consumer confidence levels in the United States conducted by the University of Michigan.
Conference Board’s Index of Consumer Confidence measures what consumers are feeling about their expected financial situation, whether that's optimistic or pessimistic.
Consumer Price Index is a statistical measure that tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a representative "market basket" of everyday goods and

services.
Nasdaq Composite Index is a stock market measure that tracks the performance of more than 3,000 stocks listed on the Nasdaq exchange.
MSCI Emerging Markets c is a stock market index that tracks the performance of large-cap and mid-cap companies across 24 developing and fast-growing nations.
MSCI All-Country World Index ex USA is a market-capitalization-weighted stock market index that tracks large- and mid-cap equity performance across developed and emerging markets

worldwide, excluding the United States.
MSCI Europe, Australasia, and the Far East (EAFE) Index is a stock market index that measures the performance of large- and mid-cap companies across 21 developed.
Dow Jones Industrial Average Index is a stock market index that tracks 30 major public companies listed on the New York Stock Exchange (NYSE) and Nasdaq.
S&P 500 Equal-Weighted Index is an alternative version of the standard S&P 500 that gives every constituent company an identical weight of 0.2% during each quarterly rebalance, rather

than weighting companies by their market capitalization.
Russell 2000 Index is a stock market index that tracks the performance of approximately 2,000 small-cap (small-capitalization) public companies in the United States.
S&P 500 sub-sectors and industry groups Global Industry Classification Standard (GICS®)/S&P 500 Total Return Index, including Information Technology Total Return (TR) USD;
Consumer Discretionary TR USD; Industrials TR USD; Real Estate TR USD; Communication Services TR USD; Materials  TR USD; Financials TR USD; Consumer Staples TR USD; Utilities TR USD;

Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

ADVANCED ECONOMIES
EUROPEAN UNION
Stronger trade posture pays off. The EU and China have agreed to
restrain hybrid cars exports to the EU to a level half or less than cur -
rently projected over the next four years, cutting exports by “several
million units” though still allowing them to rise. China also agreed to

drop its tariffs on EUR 4 bn of EU exports and provide easier access to
Chinese rare earths and magnets under a fast-track licensing sche -
me. These announcements were made at the conclusion of EU Trade
Commissioner Maroš Šefcovic’s visit to Beijing, following a recent es -
calation in trade tensions. Meanwhile, France and Germany released
a joint non-paper on Strengthening European resilience, addressing
global macroeconomic imbalances and market distorting practices

calling notably for:
i) Broadening the tools the European Commission may use to address
unfair trade practices (including undervalued exchange rates) or im -
port surges;
ii) Procedural changes to make them easier to use (qualified majority
of member states needed to block rather than authorise them). These
measures will be discussed during the next EU council on October
15-16th.
The European Commission also unveiled a new critical materials pac -

kage involving 46 strategic extraction, processing or recycling projects
spread across 16 member states (including 5 in France). The goal is to
reduce the EU’s dependence on China for critical metals.
EUROZONE
Price pressures are broadening, activity holds up. Producer price in -
flation accelerated further in August (8.3% y/y 1 from 5.8%), driven by
energy. Pressures are becoming more widespread, notably in capital
goods and above all intermediate goods (6.8%). They are partly offset

by non-durable consumer goods, which remain in deflation (-0.8%).
Given the PPI's close correlation with the HICP , this points to further
upside risks to consumer inflation. On the activity front, final September
PMIs confirmed the flash estimates, with the composite index still solid
at 53.1 and the manufacturing index stable at 52.7. The construction
PMI improved but remains depressed (43.4; +0.4 pt). Retail sales edged
up 0.1% m/m2 in August.

France
Modest spread relief as budget positioning starts. The spread between
French and German 10Y yields narrowed to 136 bps 3 on October 9
(mostly thanks to a decline in the French yield). As the Finance Com -
mittee of the National Assembly started discussing the 2027 budget,
the government reiterated its strong intent not to deviate from the
5% of GDP deficit target and to get a budget adopted in time, likely
without a vote. This is the most likely outcome — but not to be im -

plemented before 10 December (given mandatory timelines) — which
effectively removes any incentive for MPs to back unpopular measures
during the process. We can therefore expect heated debates (including
multiple no-confidence votes being tabled but rejected) and a lack in
fiscal discipline. In parallel, the "Rassemblement National" party ("Na-
tional Rally" in English) presented its fiscal strategy, which includes an
alternative budget for 2027 and an aggressive fiscal consolidation path

throughout 2032. The party called for a referendum to constitutionalise
1 Year-on-year
2 Month-on-month
3 Basis points
a “golden rule” that would require a deficit at least 0.5 pp below the
debt-stabilising threshold (roughly 3% of GDP), as long as the public
debt ratio exceeds 60% of GDP . The golden rule would require a budget
balance adjustment of 0.5 pp per year if the fiscal deficit is more than
1 pp above the stabilising threshold. The adjustment measures put

forward lack specifics but likely overstate savings, understate costs
and rely on overly optimistic growth assumptions (despite large fiscal
consolidation, at least 1.7% annually over the next three years — a
pace reached only 4 times in France since 2008, apart from post-pan-
demic reopening, and well in excess of the roughly 1% growth potential
estimated by most international institutions). That said, it is significant
that this party has chosen to fly the flag for fiscal discipline loud and

clear. Other parties should do the same.
Activity is holding up. New vehicle registrations rose 11% y/y in Sep -
tember, confirming a marked acceleration over the past three months
(+9.4% y/y in Q3). In Q3, growth came entirely from electric vehicles
(+112% y/y), whose share of registrations reached an all-time high
(38% in Q3, 41% in September). Export growth continued in August,
both in services (+5.2% y/y YTD) and in goods (+5.6% y/y YTD) — though

driven by prices rather than volume in the latter case. Manufacturing
output posted only a moderate rebound in August (+0.3% m/m; fol -
lowing cumulative declines of 2.8% over the previous three months),
limited by renewed supply constraints in Q3 (which limited produc -
tion at 31.5% of companies; +5 pp compared to H1). Industrial produc-
tion fell (-0.3% m/m) as a result of a decline in electricity production
(-2.4% m/m) driven by the heatwave. Credit growth remains dynamic.

Bank and market financing to NFCs saw steady growth in August (+4%).
New housing loans rebounded slightly in August (EUR 12.2 bn) fol -
lowing a relatively weak July, whilst interest rates continued to rise.
Consumer credit remained buoyant.
Upcoming: Banque de France monthly Business Survey (Monday), Bu-
siness creations in September (Friday).
Germany
Bullish official growth forecast upgrade despite mixed data. The
German government raised its 2026 GDP growth forecast to 1.3%

— the fastest pace since 2017 — from a prior estimate of 0.5%, citing
stronger-than-expected exports and a surge in government spending.
The 2027 forecast was also raised to 1.1% from 0.9%. Industrial pro -
duction was stronger than expected in August (+2.3% y/y versus -1.7%
in July; the highest level since March 2025 and higher than antici -
pated), driven mainly by strong growth in construction output, prima -
rily in public works (+10.5%; to the highest level since November 2021).

By contrast, manufacturing output fell by -0.2% (after +3.3% in July)
due to a decline in transport equipment production (lowest level since
April 2022). Likewise, new factory orders hit their lowest level in one
year in August (-10.6% m/m). However, this decline is mainly due
to a base effect. Export growth remains solid: +4.4% YTD, despite a
-0.8% m/m decline in August. Exports to the Eurozone continue to rise
(+6.3% y/y YTD), while exports to the U.S. have returned to positive

territory (+1.1%) and those to China continue to decline (-12.9%). The
trade surplus reached EUR 19.5 bn in August (+4% y/y YTD, despite a
-9.8% m/m decline).

UNITED STATES
Mild slowdown in services and more rate hikes signaled. The non-ma-
nufacturing ISM survey fell 0.5 pp4 to 54.9 in September due to a sharp
slowdown in the business activity component (down 5.2 pp to 56.5 pp,
still a robust expansion). Employment rose and supply pressures in -
tensified for the first time since June, while the price gauge reached

its highest level (74, up 1.4 pp) since 2022. Meanwhile, the minutes
of the September FOMC meeting confirmed the Fed’s hawkish outlook
and noted that the AI-buildout carried growing weight in the reading of
current inflation data and outlook. Governor Waller and St. Louis Fed
President Musalem mentioned the need for additional rate hikes while
remaining flexible on timing. The New York Fed survey of consumer in-
flation expectations showed them rising to 3.9%. We continue to expect

two more hikes, the next one coming in December.
Upcoming: September CPI (Wednesday), September PPI and Retail
Sales (Thursday), September Industrial Production (Friday).
JAPAN
Solid wage growth fails to lift household spending. Total wage growth
eased to 3.8% y/y in August (down 0.5 pp) as bonus payments stalled
(0% y/y, after 5.3% y/y in July). This allowed continued real wage growth
at 1.5% y/y, extending an uninterrupted run of positive readings in 2026

as administrative measures kept inflation in check. However, this did
not prevent real household spending from contracting in August for the
ninth consecutive month (-3.1% y/y).
EMERGING MARKETS
The IIF reports that nonresident portfolio flows to EMs turned negative
at -USD 26.3 bn in September, after USD 11.4 bn in August, the first net
outflow since June, as a hawkish Fed lifted US real yields and the USD.
Equity outflows reached -USD 19.2 bn, led by heavy foreign selling of

Korean equities, while debt flows turned negative at -USD 7.0 bn as
hard currency demand faded and EM credit spreads widened late in
the month. Further hikes from DM central banks will create additional
headwinds, but EM should remain resilient as long as global growth
persists.
ASIA
China
Yuan policy defence. Coinciding with the Beijing visit of the EU trade
commissioner, the People's Bank of China issued an unusually lengthy
formal statement rejecting claims that the yuan is undervalued and

defending its exchange-rate management by reiterating that it has a
‘floating exchange rate regime’ with reference to a basket of currencies.
It further argued that China's trade surpluses are driven by rising in -
dustrial competitiveness rather than currency under-devaluation and
that exchange-rate adjustments will not resolve structural global im -
balances. Nonetheless, it allowed the yuan to hit a four-year high vs.
the CFETS basket — bringing its year-to-date gain to 5.7%. The PBOC

set the USD/CNY's reference rate at 6.7330, the strongest level since
2023.
India
Monetary tightening. The RBI raised its repo rate by 25bp to 5.5%, its
first hike since February 2023, and shifted to a “calibrated tightening”
stance amidst rising inflationary pressures. The RBI introduced new
measures to stabilise the rupee, including direct dollar supplies to
state-owned oil companies and tighter restrictions on FX derivatives.
4 Percentage points

EUROPE, MIDDLE EAST, AFRICA
Central Europe
Cautious monetary policy stance amid mild inflation uptick. Poland’s
and Romania’s Central banks kept policy rates unchanged at 3.75% and
6.5%, respectively. Inflation accelerated in September, with a mild up-
tick in the Czech Republic and Hungary (respectively 2.5% and 1.6% y/y)
and a more entrenched increase in Poland (4% y/y). Inflation is now
above the official target in the Czech Rep. and Poland, but fuel price

caps in both countries should contain inflation dynamics.
Gulf
PMIs for September point for a firm but uneven non-oil recovery. Both
Saudi and UAE PMIs stood at 55.3, driven mostly by export orders in
the UAE and by domestic demand in Saudi Arabia. On the contrary,
Qatar’s PMI fell again (to 46.7), while Kuwait’s PMI also declined but
remained in expansionary zone (at 52.4).
Türkiye
Manufacturing production contracted by 1.6% m/m in August, the lar-

gest monthly decrease since the beginning of the year. On a y/y ba -
sis, it fell by 2.3%. Both domestic demand and exports seem to have
contributed negatively to this poor outcome. On a positive note, in
September, CPI inflation (1.9% m/m, 27.9% y/y) slowed more than the
market expected, and exports rebounded.
LATIN AMERICA
Inflationary pressures slightly up across the region. In Brazil, headline
CPI inflation accelerated to 4.6% y/y from 4.2% y/y in August (above the

central bank's 4.5% target), partly reflecting the reversal of a tempora-
ry electricity price discount in August. In Mexico, inflation rose to 3.5%
in September from 3.3% in August, while in Colombia it reached 6.3%,
slightly up from 6.2% in August. Inflation remained high in Chile but
was stable at 4.1%. In Peru, it was also slightly up (4.2% vs. 4.1%); the
central bank kept its policy rate unchanged at 4.25% for the thirteenth
consecutive month but maintained an upward bias amid rising inflation

and energy prices.
COMMODITIES
Energy: Limited relief. Continuation of skirmishes in the Gulf of Hormuz
and escalation of the conflict between the Houthis and a Saudi-led
coalition left oil prices fluctuating around $100/b for Brent. However,
the complex security situation has been driving the cost of shipping to
record high levels and the price of physical barrel to early April levels.
Late on Friday, President Trump announced an agreement with Russian

President Putin to release to global markets 4.8 million tonnes of die -
sel (roughly 8 days of European consumption), provided conditions in
refineries allow for it, of which 300,000 tonnes immediately. The White
House also announced a suspension of sanctions on Russia’s exports
of diesel until April 2027. Energy experts expect a de minimis price
impact.
European gas prices (TTF) rose by 11.5% over the week to 82 €/MWh.
EU gas inventories reached 73.6% of full capacities on October 11 th

(the official target is 80% at end October).
Food inflation rose sharply in September. The FAO’s Food Price Index
grew by 5.8% y/y, up from just 3% in August. This was driven by cereal
(+17% y/y) and sugar prices (+15%).

We've managed to get through a week without mapping out

That's great, but we'd not get too comfortable. Many of the

developments in the Ukraine and Iran wars make for diﬃcult

Padhraic Garvey, CFA
The mood for US Treasuries has calmed.
But don't get too comfortable
Long-end yields have calmed in the past week or so. But pressures

remain. The medium-term issuance narrative stemming from ﬁscal
largesse remains. That’s the 'bad'. Then there’s the 'ugly', in the
guise of energy price pressure stemming from the Iran war. That
has coincided with higher printed inﬂation. Arguably, this is the
biggest direct impact, as it has prompted central bank rate hikes.
And there is the 'good'. Higher real rates have been an important
driver, and a part of this likely incorporates AI-driven productivity

growth. If that last part was the central driver, we’d have little to

worry about. But it’s the other, more sinister factors that have
predominantly sparked long-end market jitters.
The balance of probabilities suggests there is at least one more leg
of long-end weakness still to play out. This could take the US 10-
year yield back into the 5.25% to 5.5% range. Other markets would
likely move in tandem, mapping out similar, albeit less dramatic,
blow-outs. With 5.25% looking like a mean-reversion level, we think
the US 10-year yield could overshoot towards 5.5% and remain

elevated for a number of months as bond market jitters persist.
This could quite easily extend into the ﬁrst quarter of 2027.
For later in 2027, we think inﬂation will come down, and long
rates should too. On the growth side, we have a 2% real
growth forecast tendency for 2027. If we’re wrong and we’re
on the verge of a macro boom (and/or persistently elevated
inﬂation), that’s a whole other story, and it would be a route
to 5.5-6%. The other way to get there could be the US ﬁscal

story ﬁnally unravelling in a bad way, but we aren't quite
sure we can call for that yet as a baseline view.
For 2027, we have growth of 2% and inﬂation of 2% (or
slightly above on both counts). That points to 4.75%,
representing a mild upside bias relative to the 4.5% level we
view as broadly neutral. But ﬁrst, we need to get through
2026.

This is probably overdone, as a swing to the right (should it occur)
could have some tail positives that aren't currently properly
discounted. Look at the improvement seen in Italian spreads
following the rise of Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni. It's been gradual

concerns. In that sense, the Italian response has been low beta in
characterisation, which is good.
In fact, the ﬁrst real bout of widening in French spreads can be

traced back to President Emmanuel Macron's election gamble of
mid-2024. Things subsequently calmed, but were not forgotten.
The recent spike is an ampliﬁcation and extension of an underlying
tension that has not gone away. So, overdone? Probably, yes. But
we also remain in a danger zone for bonds generally. Just like in the
US, more overshoots to the upside are entirely foreseeable.

## Document 349: United States _ A shock-resistant expansion (2026-09-24)

Source PDF: all report pdf/United States _ A shock-resistant expansion (2026-09-24).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-24
Pages: 2
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

6
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
A SHOCK-RESISTANT EXPANSION
US growth remains resilient despite successive shocks. According to our forecasts, it is expected to reach 2.2% in 2026 and
2.3% in 2027. Its main driver is still investment in artificial intelligence (AI), which has remained entirely unaffected by the
energy crisis. The labour market has seen a marked improvement in job creation and a fall in the unemployment rate. Core

inflation remains stubbornly high and above the Federal Reserve (Fed)’s target. Against this backdrop, the Fed has begun a
hiking cycle in September, which, according to our scenario, would bring the Fed Funds target to 4.50% (upper limit) by Q1
2027. At the same time, long-term rates are under upward pressure.
1  This includes: energy and communications; data centres (facilities); IT equipment and peripherals; communication equipment (equipment); software; and research and develop-

ment (intellectual-property products).
THE ENERGY SHOCK IS NOT HOLDING BACK GROWTH
Driven by AI-related investment, US growth is holding up despite ri -
sing energy inflation. We forecast GDP growth at 2.2% in 2026 and
2.3% in 2027 on an annual average basis. The AI-related investment
cycle1 (+14.8% y/y in H1 2026) is expected to continue, accompanied by
potential productivity gains. However, other private-investment seg -
ments (including residential property) remain sluggish. In addition, the

rising cost of capital could accentuate its ‘K-shaped’ profile. Household
consumption is expected, on the whole, to benefit from the rebound in
job creation and to remain above 2% y/y through to the end of 2027.
In H1 2026, it held up against resurging inflation due to an average
year-on-year increase of 11.5% in tax refunds and a savings rate stan-
ding at its lowest level since 2022 (2.8% in Q2).
RECORD CAPITAL-RAISING DRIVEN BY AI
This year, AI-related investments are driving massive capital-raising

by companies through loans, bond issues or share placements. This
trend is set to continue given the seemingly colossal scale of the fun -
ding requirements, even as risk premia in the bond market begin to
widen. According to their statements, the five tech giants could invest
nearly USD 900 bn this year and over USD 1,000 bn next year—more
than double the figure for 2025. Spurred on by regulatory easing, the
major US banks have announced that they will mobilise significant re-

sources to support projects considered strategic for the United States
(AI, defence, energy and infrastructure). While funding for the AI boom
appears unaffected by rising long-term interest rates, these latter are
likely to continue to weigh on household demand for mortgages. Since
the start of the conflict in the Middle East, 30-year mortgage rates
have risen by 78 bp (to 6.76% at the start of September). Conversely,
consumer lending remained buoyant in H1 2026 and is supporting

household spending.
TWO-SPEED GROWTH IN INDUSTRY
Industrial production rebounded slightly in 2025 (+1.1%), driven by AI
and aerospace. We forecast an acceleration to +1.5% in 2026 and +2%
in 2027. Investment in AI is bolstering the electronics sector, while the
(civil and military) aerospace sector is rebounding as order backlogs
are cleared and defence requirements rise. Conversely, sectors ex -
posed to rising tariffs (in particular, the steel, aluminium and auto -

motive sectors) are still on the back burner, contrary to the objective
of these measures. This positive momentum in industrial production,
while concentrated, is expected to continue, bolstered by the recove -
ry in business sentiment. The ISM Manufacturing Index has been in
expansionary territory since early 2026, despite ongoing pressures on
prices and supply chains.
